\lvert\psi(t)\rangle=\mathcal{T}\exp\left(-\mathrm{i}\int_{0}^{t}H(s)\,ds\right)\lvert\psi(0)\rangle.

(266)

Here

\partial_{t}\mathcal{T}\exp\left(-\mathrm{i}\int_{0}^{t}H(s)\,ds\right)=-\mathrm{i}H(t)\mathcal{T}\exp\left(-\mathrm{i}\int_{0}^{t}H(s)\,ds\right).

(267)

We now consider the error due to the discretization of this integral.
In each short time interval, we have

\int_{\tau}^{\tau+\delta}H(s)\,ds\approx H(\tau)\delta\,.

(268)

We can approximate
H
within the interval by

H(s)\approx H(\tau)+(s-\tau)H^{\prime}(\tau).

(269)

The error is upper bounded by

\left\|H(\tau)-H(s)\right\|\leq\delta\max_{v\in[\tau,\tau+\delta]}\|H^{\prime}(v)\|.

(270)

Then we can use the triangle inequality on unitary operators in the form

\displaystyle\|ABC-A^{\prime}B^{\prime}C^{\prime}\|

\displaystyle=\|ABC-A^{\prime}BC+A^{\prime}BC-A^{\prime}B^{\prime}C+A^{\prime}B^{\prime}C-A^{\prime}B^{\prime}C^{\prime}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|(A-A^{\prime})BC\|+\|A^{\prime}(B-B^{\prime})C\|+\|A^{\prime}B^{\prime}(C-C^{\prime})\|

\displaystyle=\|A-A^{\prime}\|+\|B-B^{\prime}\|+\|C-C^{\prime}\|.

(271)

That means, for the time evolution, which is a product of evolutions for infinitesimal time periods, we can bound the difference by the integral of the differences between the Hamiltonians.
So, if we have Hamiltonians
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
, then

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{T}\exp\left(-\mathrm{i}\int_{0}^{t}H_{1}(s)\,ds\right)-\mathcal{T}\exp\left(-\mathrm{i}\int_{0}^{t}H_{2}(s)\,ds\right)\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{\tau}\mathcal{T}\exp\left(-\mathrm{i}\int_{0}^{\tau}H_{1}(s)\,ds\right)\mathrm{d}\tau-\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{\tau}\mathcal{T}\exp\left(-\mathrm{i}\int_{0}^{\tau}H_{2}(s)\,ds\right)\mathrm{d}\tau\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\int_{0}^{t}(-\mathrm{i}H_{1}(\tau))\mathcal{T}\exp\left(-\mathrm{i}\int_{0}^{\tau}H_{1}(s)\,ds\right)\mathrm{d}\tau-\int_{0}^{t}(-\mathrm{i}H_{2}(\tau))\mathcal{T}\exp\left(-\mathrm{i}\int_{0}^{\tau}H_{2}(s)\,ds\right)\mathrm{d}\tau\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\|H_{1}(s)-H_{2}(s)\|\,ds.

(272)

In the case of the interaction picture, we have

H(t)=e^{\mathrm{i}tA}Be^{-\mathrm{i}tA},

(273)

and thus the product rule implies

H^{\prime}(t)=\mathrm{i}\,e^{\mathrm{i}tA}[A,B]e^{-\mathrm{i}tA}.

(274)

As a result, using the triangle inequality and sub-multiplicative property of the spectral norm we can bound
\|H^{\prime}(t)\|
as

\|H^{\prime}(t)\|\leq 2\|A\|\,\|B\|.

(275)

For a single time step of length
\delta
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\tau}^{\tau+\delta}\left\|H(\tau)-H(s)\right\|ds

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\tau}^{\tau+\delta}|s-\tau|2\|A\|\,\|B\|\,ds

\displaystyle=\delta^{2}\|A\|\,\|B\|.

(276)

Therefore, if the integral is approximated by
M
time steps of size
\delta=\tau/M
, then the error in the integral is bounded by

M(\tau/M)^{2}\|A\|\,\|B\|=\frac{\tau^{2}}{M}\|A\|\,\|B\|.

(277)

As we are working in the interaction frame of the kinetic operator,
\|A\|
can be upper bounded by
\lambda_{T}
, and
\|B\|
upper bounded by
\lambda_{U}+\lambda_{V}
.
We are block encoding the Dyson series for the time-evolution operator over a time interval
\tau=1/(\lambda_{U}+\lambda_{V})
, and the number of time steps is (by the definition of
n_{t}
)
M=2^{n_{t}}
.
As a result, the error in the block encoded operator due to time discretization is no larger than

\frac{\tau^{2}}{M}\|A\|\,\|B\|\leq\frac{\lambda_{T}}{2^{n_{t}}(\lambda_{U}+\lambda_{V})}.

(278)

This error translates to an error in the estimated eigenvalue of
H/(\lambda_{U}+\lambda_{V})
.
Therefore, if
\epsilon_{t}
is the allowable error due to the time discretization, then we need

\lambda_{T}2^{-n_{t}}\leq\epsilon_{t}.

(279)

Therefore,
n_{t}
should be chosen as

n_{t}=\lceil\log(\lambda_{T}/\epsilon_{t})\rceil.

(280)

Note that, in the above, the approximation of the time-dependent Hamiltonian is made at one side of the interval.
A more accurate approximation can be made by using the middle of the interval, though that only reduces the number of bits for the time by 1 in this analysis.
A more careful analysis can be made where the second-order correction to the Hamiltonian is considered.
That can be used to show that the error in the approximation of the integral over the Hamiltonian is higher order.

Consider
U_{1}(\delta)
to be the time evolution operator over the interval
[\tau-\delta/2,\tau+\delta/2)
for the Hamiltonian
H
, and
U_{0}(\delta)
to be the time evolution operator for the Hamiltonian in the center of the interval,
H(\tau)
. We can find an expression for the derivative of
U_{1}(\delta)
by integrating the differential equation for the time-evolution operator. As appropriate, we will also use the notation that the time evolution operator from times
a
to
b
is
U_{1}(b,a)
.
Then we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{\delta}U_{1}(\delta)

\displaystyle=\partial_{\delta}(U(\tau+\delta/2,\tau)U(\tau,\tau-\delta/2))

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}H(\tau+\delta/2)U_{1}(\delta)+U(\tau+\delta/2,\tau)(\partial_{\delta}U(\tau,\tau-\delta/2))

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}H(\tau+\delta/2)U_{1}(\delta)+U(\tau+\delta/2,\tau)(\partial_{\delta}U^{\dagger}(\tau-\delta/2,\tau))

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}H(\tau+\delta/2)U_{1}(\delta)+U(\tau+\delta/2,\tau)(\partial_{\delta}U(\tau-\delta/2,\tau))^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}[H(\tau+\delta/2)U_{1}(\delta)+U_{1}(\delta)H(\tau-\delta/2)].

(281)

Next, it follows immediately that the derivative of
U_{0}
is

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\delta}U_{0}(\delta)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\left[H(\tau)U_{0}(\delta)+U_{0}(\delta)H(\tau)\right].

(282)

We can then upper bound the rate of change of the norm of the difference of
U_{0}
and
U_{1}
by

\displaystyle\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h}\big|\|U_{1}(\delta+h)-U_{0}(\delta+h)\|-\|U_{1}(\delta)-U_{0}(\delta)\|\big|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{h}\left[U_{1}(\delta+h)-U_{0}(\delta+h)-U_{1}(\delta)-U_{0}(\delta)\right]\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{\partial}{\partial\delta}[U_{1}(\delta)-U_{0}(\delta)]\right\|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|U_{1}(\delta)H(\tau-\delta/2)+H(\tau+\delta/2)U_{1}(\delta)-U_{0}(\delta)H(\tau)-H(\tau)U_{0}(\delta)\|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Biggr\|U_{1}(\delta)H(\tau-\delta/2)+U_{1}(\delta)H(\tau+\delta/2)-2U_{1}(\delta)H(\tau)

\displaystyle\quad-U_{1}(\delta)H(\tau+\delta/2)+U_{1}(\delta)H(\tau)+H(\tau+\delta/2)U_{1}(\delta)-H(\tau)U_{1}(\delta)

\displaystyle\quad+U_{1}(\delta)H(\tau)-U_{0}(\delta)H(\tau)+H(\tau)U_{1}(\delta)-H(\tau)U_{0}(\delta)\Biggr\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\Biggr(\|U_{1}(\delta)[H(\tau-\delta/2)+H(\tau+\delta/2)-2H(\tau)]\|

\displaystyle\quad+\|[H(\tau+\delta/2)-H(\tau),U_{1}(\delta)]\|

\displaystyle\quad+\|(U_{1}(\delta)-U_{0}(\delta))H(\tau)\|+\|H(\tau)(U_{1}(\delta)-U_{0}(\delta))\|\Biggr).

(283)

Using an upper bound
\Lambda
on
\|H\|
, we have that
\|U(\delta)-I\|\leq\delta\Lambda
.
We can therefore say that

\displaystyle\|[H(\tau+\delta/2)-H(\tau),U_{1}(\delta)]\|

\displaystyle=\|[H(\tau+\delta/2)-H(\tau),U_{1}(\delta)-I]\|

\displaystyle\leq 2\|U_{1}(\delta)-I\|\,\|H(\tau+\delta/2)-H(\tau)\|

\displaystyle\leq 2\delta\Lambda\|H(\tau+\delta/2)-H(\tau)\|.

(284)

If there is an upper limit
\Gamma_{1}
on the norm of the derivative of
H
, then

\|[H(\tau+\delta/2)-H(\tau),U_{1}(\delta)]\|\leq\delta^{2}\Lambda\Gamma_{1}.

(285)

If there is a constant upper bound
\Gamma_{2}
on the second derivative of
H
, we get

\|U_{1}(\delta)[H(\tau+\delta/2)+H(\tau-\delta/2)-2H(\tau)]\|\leq\Gamma_{2}\delta^{2}/4.

(286)

These bounds give us

\left\|U_{1}(\delta)-U_{0}(\delta)\right\|\leq\int_{0}^{\delta}\left\{\frac{1}{2}(\Lambda\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}/4)s^{2}+\Lambda\left\|U_{1}(s)-U_{0}(s)\right\|\right\}\mathrm{d}s.

(287)

A solution to the above integral equation is

\left\|U_{1}(\delta)-U_{0}(\delta)\right\|\leq\frac{4\Lambda\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}}{(2\Lambda)^{3}}\left[2\left(e^{\Lambda\delta}-1\right)-\Lambda\delta(2+\Lambda\delta)\right].

(288)

For the interaction picture, we have

\displaystyle H(t)

\displaystyle=e^{\mathrm{i}tA}Be^{-\mathrm{i}tA},

(289)

\displaystyle H^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}e^{\mathrm{i}tA}[A,B]e^{-\mathrm{i}tA},

(290)

\displaystyle H^{\prime\prime}(t)

\displaystyle=-e^{\mathrm{i}tA}[A,[A,B]]e^{-\mathrm{i}tA}.

(291)

Therefore we can take
\Lambda=\|B\|
,
\Gamma_{1}=2\|A\|\,\|B\|
and
\Gamma_{2}=4\|A\|^{2}\|B\|
, so we get

\left\|U_{1}(\delta)-U_{0}(\delta)\right\|\leq\left(\frac{\|A\|}{\|B\|}+\frac{\|A\|^{2}}{2\|B\|^{2}}\right)\left[2\left(e^{\|B\|\delta}-1\right)-\|B\|\delta(2+\|B\|\delta)\right].

(292)

For our application, we are approximating the integral by
2^{n_{t}}
time steps of size
\delta=\tau/2^{n_{t}}
.
Then the error in the time-ordered exponential over time step
\delta
is bounded by

2^{-n_{t}}\left(\frac{2\|A\|}{\|B\|}+\frac{\|A\|^{2}}{\|B\|^{2}}\right)\left[2^{n_{t}}\left(e^{\|B\|\tau/2^{n_{t}}}-1\right)-\|B\|\tau(1+\|B\|\tau/2^{n_{t}+1})\right].

(293)

Multiplying by
2^{n_{t}}
gives the bound on the error for the time-ordered exponential over the entire region

\left(\frac{2\|A\|}{\|B\|}+\frac{\|A\|^{2}}{\|B\|^{2}}\right)\left[2^{n_{t}}\left(e^{\|B\|\tau/2^{n_{t}}}-1\right)-\|B\|\tau(1+\|B\|\tau/2^{n_{t}+1})\right].

(294)

This gives us the error in approximating the time-evolution operator with the centered difference formula and this leads us to the following lemma.

Lemma 11
.

Let
H(t)=e^{\mathrm{i}tA}Be^{-\mathrm{i}tA}
for bounded Hermitian operators
A
and
B
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
. We then have that if
n_{t}
is a positive integer and
\delta=\tau/2^{n_{t}}

then

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}e^{-\mathrm{i}\int_{t_{0}-\delta/2}^{t_{0}+\delta/2}H(t)\mathrm{d}t}-e^{-\mathrm{i}H({t_{0}})\delta}\|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 2^{-n_{t}}\left(\frac{2\|A\|}{\|B\|}+\frac{\|A\|^{2}}{\|B\|^{2}}\right)\left[2^{n_{t}}\left(e^{\|B\|\tau/2^{n_{t}}}-1\right)-\|B\|\tau(1+\|B\|\tau/2^{n_{t}+1})\right].

In practice, for the interaction picture one should have
\|A\|\ll\|B\|
, so that it is the second term given in the round brackets above that should be dominant.
Moreover, expanding the exponential gives

2\left(e^{\Lambda\delta}-1\right)-\Lambda\delta(2+\Lambda\delta)=\frac{1}{3}(\Lambda\delta)^{3}+\mathcal{O}((\Lambda\delta)^{4}).

(295)

Therefore, we expect that the error should be approximately

\left\|U_{1}(\delta)-U_{0}(\delta)\right\|\lesssim\frac{\delta^{3}\|A\|^{2}\|B\|}{6}

(296)

for a time step, or

\frac{\tau^{3}\|A\|^{2}\|B\|}{6\times 2^{2n_{t}}},

(297)

for the entire time interval.

A similar result can be obtained for the approximation of just the integral by the discretized integral.

\left\|H(\tau)+(s-\tau)H^{\prime}(\tau)-H(s)\right\|\leq\frac{(s-\tau)^{2}}{2}\max_{v\in[\tau-\delta/2,\tau+\delta/2]}\|H^{\prime\prime}(v)\|.

(298)

The bound on the difference between the integral over time
\delta
and the value of
H
is therefore

\displaystyle\left\|H(\tau)\delta-\int_{\tau-\delta/2}^{\tau+\delta/2}H(s)\,ds\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\tau-\delta/2}^{\tau+\delta/2}\frac{(s-\tau)^{2}}{2}4\|A\|^{2}\|B\|\,ds

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta^{3}}{24}4\|A\|^{2}\|B\|=\frac{\delta^{3}}{6}\|A\|^{2}\|B\|.

(299)

In taking the time-ordered exponential of the integral, we cannot simply use this bound because the term
(s-\tau)H^{\prime}(\tau)
does not average out, and there are contributions from higher-order terms in the exponential.
The above derivation is able to take account of that.

Appendix I
Interaction picture algorithm for simulating generic Hamiltonians

We have given an implementation of the interaction picture algorithm in
Section
III
for simulating first quantized quantum chemistry. In this appendix, we describe a related implementation for simulating a generic Hamiltonian.

As in
Section
III
, we assume that the target Hamiltonian takes the form
H=A+B
, where
\left\lVert B\right\rVert
is much smaller than
\left\lVert A\right\rVert
. Then, the evolution under
H
for a short time
\tau
can be represented by the Dyson series as

\displaystyle e^{-\mathrm{i}(A+B)\tau}

\displaystyle=e^{-\mathrm{i}\tau A}\mathcal{T}\exp\left(-\mathrm{i}\int_{0}^{\tau}ds\,e^{isA}Be^{-\mathrm{i}sA}\right)

(300)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-\mathrm{i})^{k}}{k!}\int_{0}^{\tau}d\tau_{1}\int_{0}^{\tau}d\tau_{2}\cdots\int_{0}^{\tau}d\tau_{k}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}(\tau-\tau^{\prime}_{k})A}Be^{-\mathrm{i}(\tau^{\prime}_{k}-\tau^{\prime}_{k-1})A}B\ldots Be^{-\mathrm{i}(\tau^{\prime}_{2}-\tau^{\prime}_{1})A}Be^{-\mathrm{i}\tau_{1}A}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\rightarrow\infty\\
M\rightarrow\infty\end{subarray}}\sum_{k=0}^{K}\frac{(-\mathrm{i}\tau)^{k}}{M^{k}k!}\sum_{m_{1}=0}^{M-1}\sum_{m_{2}=0}^{M-1}\cdots\sum_{m_{k}=0}^{M-1}e^{-\mathrm{i}\tau(M-1/2-m^{\prime}_{k})A/M}Be^{-\mathrm{i}\tau(m^{\prime}_{k}-m^{\prime}_{k-1})A/M}B\ldots

\displaystyle\quad\ldots Be^{-\mathrm{i}\tau(m^{\prime}_{2}-m^{\prime}_{1})A/M}Be^{-\mathrm{i}\tau(m^{\prime}_{1}+1/2)A/M}

where
\tau^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\tau^{\prime}_{k}
are sorted times from
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{k}
, and
m^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,m^{\prime}_{k}
are sorted integers from
m_{1},\ldots,m_{k}
. Here, we assume that the operator
B
is given by the block encoding

\langle 0\rvert\mathrm{PREP}_{B}^{\dagger}\cdot\mathrm{SEL}_{B}\cdot\mathrm{PREP}_{B}\lvert 0\rangle=B/\lambda_{B},

(301)

and
A
can be directly exponentiated on a quantum computer. This gives the linear combination of unitaries

\displaystyle e^{-\mathrm{i}(A+B)\tau}\approx\left(\langle 0\rvert\mathrm{PREP}_{B}^{\dagger}\right)^{\otimes K}\sum_{k=0}^{K}\frac{(\lambda_{B}\tau)^{k}}{M^{k}k!}\sum_{m_{1},...,m_{k}=0}^{M-1}\bigg(e^{-\mathrm{i}\tau(M-1/2-m^{\prime}_{k})A/M}\left(-\mathrm{i}\mathrm{SEL}_{B}\right)e^{-\mathrm{i}\tau(m^{\prime}_{k}-m^{\prime}_{k-1})A/M}

\displaystyle\quad\times\left(-\mathrm{i}\mathrm{SEL}_{B}\right)\ldots\ldots\left(-\mathrm{i}\mathrm{SEL}_{B}\right)e^{-\mathrm{i}\tau(m^{\prime}_{2}-m^{\prime}_{1})A/M}\left(-\mathrm{i}\mathrm{SEL}_{B}\right)e^{-\mathrm{i}\tau(m^{\prime}_{1}+1/2)A/M}\bigg)\left(\mathrm{PREP}_{B}\lvert 0\rangle\right)^{\otimes K},

(302)

where
\mathrm{PREP}_{B}
prepares initial states in
K
different ancilla registers and, for each fixed value of
k
, the
k
selection operators
\mathrm{SEL}_{B}
act on
k
different registers and the target system register.

To implement this on a quantum computer, we first prepare the state that encodes the coefficients from the Dyson-series expansion

\frac{1}{\sqrt{\beta}}\sum_{k=0}^{K}\sqrt{\frac{(\lambda_{B}\tau)^{k}}{k!}}\lvert k\rangle,

(303)

where
\beta
is the normalization factor and
\lvert k\rangle
is represented in unary as
\lvert 1^{k}0^{K-k}\rangle
. We would like to prepare this state in a similar way as in
Section
III.4
. To this end, we assume that the time interval
\tau
can be taken as a rational multiple of
1/\lambda_{B}
:

\tau=\frac{t}{\mathcal{N}}=\frac{c}{d\lambda_{B}},

(304)

where
\mathcal{N}
is the number of simulation steps and
c
and
d
are positive integers that are relatively prime.
We can see that the squared amplitudes will be integers if we multiply by
\Sigma(0)
, where we redefine
\Sigma(k)
as

\Sigma(k)=\sum_{\ell=k}^{K}\frac{K!c^{\ell}d^{K-\ell}}{\ell!}.

(305)

This means that we can perform state preparation using the same inequality testing procedure as in
Section
III.4
except replacing the definition of
\Sigma(k)
.
One minor change we need to make is that the first two amplitudes (
k=0
and 1) are no longer equal.
This means we need to perform the inequality test with the number
\Sigma(k+1)
for
0\leq k<K
, with complexity
\lceil{\log\left(\Sigma(k)\right)}\rceil-1
.
For
0<k<K
we also need a Toffoli for the conditioning on the result of the previous inequality test.
Combining this with the cost in (
161
), the total Toffoli complexity for this state preparation (including inverse preparation) is

2\left(3n_{k}+2b_{r}-9\right)-1+\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\lceil{\log\left(\Sigma(k)\right)}\rceil.

(306)

where
n_{k}=\lceil{\log\left(\Sigma(0)\right)}\rceil
is the number of qubits for the equal superposition state.

Next, we consider the preparation of the register:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\beta}}\sum_{k=0}^{K}\sqrt{\frac{(\lambda_{B}\tau)^{k}}{M^{k}k!}}\lvert k\rangle\sum_{m_{1},...,m_{k}=0}^{M-1}\lvert m_{1},...,m_{k},M-1,...,M-1\rangle

(307)

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\beta}}\sum_{k=0}^{K}\sqrt{\frac{(\lambda_{B}\tau)^{k}}{M^{k}k!}}\lvert k\rangle\sum_{m_{1},...,m_{k}=0}^{M-1}\lvert m_{1}^{\prime},...,m_{k}^{\prime},M-1,...,M-1\rangle

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\beta}}\sum_{k=0}^{K}\sqrt{\frac{(\lambda_{B}\tau)^{k}}{M^{k}k!}}\lvert k\rangle\sum_{m_{1},...,m_{k}=0}^{M-1}\lvert m_{1}^{\prime},m_{2}^{\prime}-m_{1}^{\prime},...,m_{k}^{\prime}-m_{k-1}^{\prime},M-1-m_{k}^{\prime},0,...,0\rangle.

Specifically, we prepare
k
equal superposition states for each order
k
of the Dyson series using Hadamard gates controlled by the
\lvert k\rangle
register. We initialize the remaining
K-k
ancilla registers to state
\lvert M-1\rangle
corresponding to the maximal discrete time. This state preparation has Toffoli complexity
Kn_{t}
and will be inverted with the same cost. We then quantumly sort the
K
registers with cost
2n_{t}{\rm St}\left(K\right)
and compute the time differences with cost
(K-1)(n_{t}-1)
. The same complexity will be needed to invert the sorting while no Toffoli gate is required to uncompute the time differences.

At the beginning and the end we also need to apply evolution by time
\tau/2M
.
There are
(K+1)n_{t}
bits controlling evolution under
A
.
For simplicity we will combine these bits with the two uncontrolled evolutions, giving a total of
(K+1)n_{t}+2
.
Therefore we implement exponentials of
A
controlled by the time registers and perform
\mathrm{PREP}_{B}
and
\mathrm{SEL}_{B}
to block-encode
B
with cost

[(K+1)n_{t}+2]\ \textrm{controlled-}e^{isA}+2K\ \mathrm{PREP}_{B}+K\ \mathrm{SEL}_{B}.

(308)

Note that this only represents a upper bound on the cost and can often be improved by using specific properties of the target Hamiltonian; see
Section
III
for improvements for performing the first quantized quantum chemistry simulation.
This implements the interaction picture algorithm for time
\tau
.

The success amplitude of this procedure can be made larger than
1/2
. To make the amplitude equal to
1/2
, we introduce an ancilla register with state

\cos\theta\lvert 0\rangle+\sin\theta\lvert 1\rangle,

(309)

so that the success amplitude is now multiplied by
\cos\theta
. Letting
n_{\theta}
be the number of bits used to represent
\theta
, we can then prepare this state with
n_{\theta}-3
Toffoli gates.

To boost the success amplitude close to unity, we need to perform a single step of oblivious amplitude amplification, which triples the implementation of the truncated Dyson series and introduces two additional reflections. Specifically,

1.

We implement the pre-amplified truncated Dyson series and prepare the
\theta
state with a complexity as described above.

2.

We perform a reflection on
n_{k}+K\left(n_{t}+n_{B}\right)+2
qubits.

3.

We invert the truncated Dyson series and unprepare the
\theta
state.

4.

We perform a reflection again on
n_{k}+K\left(n_{t}+n_{B}\right)+2
qubits.

5.

We implement the truncated Dyson series without preparing the
\theta
state.

The
+2
accounts for the qubit rotated by
\theta
here, as well as a qubit rotated for the preparation of the equal superposition state for preparing
k
.
Each reflection can be implemented with Toffoli complexity

n_{k}+K\left(n_{t}+n_{B}\right),

(310)

where
n_{B}
is the number of ancilla qubits for block-encoding operator
B
.

We now analyze the normalization factor introduced by the above steps. Such a factor will come from three sources: (i) the preparation of the Dyson-expansion coefficients; (ii) the preparation of the equal superposition state on the
\lvert k\rangle
register; and (iii) the implementation of the block encoding of operator
B
. Same as in
Section
III.4
, we have that the success probability of Item (ii) is
p={\rm Ps}\left(\Sigma(0),b_{r}\right)
, whereas Items (i) and (iii) together contribute a normalization factor of
\beta=\sum_{k=0}^{K}c^{k}/(d^{k}k!)\leq\exp(c/d)
. To boost this close to unity with a single step of oblivious amplitude amplification, we choose coprime integers
c
and
d
so that

\frac{{\rm Ps}\left(\Sigma(0),b_{r}\right)}{\exp(c/d)}\geq\frac{1}{2},

(311)

which implies

\mathcal{N}=\frac{\lambda_{B}td}{c}.

(312)

The error of our circuit implementation is due to the truncation of the Dyson-series expansion (
\epsilon_{K}
) and the finite binary representation of the time steps (
\epsilon_{t}
), as well as the preparation of
\theta
state (
\epsilon_{\theta}
). With
n_{\theta}
digits to represent the angle
\theta
, the error in the amplitude
\cos\theta
is upper bounded by

\epsilon_{\theta}=\frac{\pi}{2^{n_{\theta}}},

(313)

which causes an overall error of at most
\epsilon_{K}+\epsilon_{\theta}
in the pre-amplified truncated Dyson series. After a single step of oblivious amplitude amplification, the error is bounded as
(
Childs et al. 2018
, Lemma G.2)

(\epsilon_{K}+\epsilon_{\theta})\frac{(\epsilon_{K}+\epsilon_{\theta})^{2}+3(\epsilon_{K}+\epsilon_{\theta})+4}{2}.

(314)

To ensure that the entire simulation has accuracy
\epsilon
, we require that

\mathcal{N}(\epsilon_{K}+\epsilon_{\theta})\frac{(\epsilon_{K}+\epsilon_{\theta})^{2}+3(\epsilon_{K}+\epsilon_{\theta})+4}{2}+\mathcal{N}\epsilon_{t}\leq\epsilon.

(315)

We summarize the cost of the circuit implementation in the following theorem.

Theorem 12
.

Using the interaction picture algorithm, the time evolution under the Hamiltonian
H=A+B
can be simulated for time
t
that is a rational multiple of
1/\lambda_{B}
to within error
\epsilon
by choosing positive integers
K
,
n_{t}
,
n_{\theta}
,
b_{r}
, coprime
c
and
d
, and
using
3[\mathcal{N}(K+1)n_{t}+2]\ \textrm{controlled-}e^{isA}
,
6\mathcal{N}K\ \mathrm{PREP}_{B}
,
3\mathcal{N}K\ \mathrm{SEL}_{B}
, and a number of Toffoli gates

\displaystyle 6\mathcal{N}(3n_{k}+2b_{r}-9)+3\mathcal{N}\left(-1+\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\lceil\log(\Sigma(k))\rceil\right)+6\mathcal{N}Kn_{t}+12\mathcal{N}(n_{t}-1){\rm St}\left(K\right)

\displaystyle+6\mathcal{N}(K-1)(n_{t}-1)+2\mathcal{N}(n_{\theta}-3)+2\mathcal{N}\left[n_{k}+K(n_{t}+n_{B})\right],

(316)

where
{\rm St}\left(K\right)
is the cost of sorting
K
items,
\tau=c/(d\lambda_{B})
,
\mathcal{N}=t/\tau
is an integer, and

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm Ps}\left(\Sigma(0),b_{r}\right)}{\exp(c/d)}\geq\frac{1}{2},

(317)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\epsilon_{K}+\epsilon_{\theta})\frac{(\epsilon_{K}+\epsilon_{\theta})^{2}+3(\epsilon_{K}+\epsilon_{\theta})+4}{2}+\mathcal{N}\epsilon_{t}\leq\epsilon,

(318)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{K}=\exp(c/d)-\sum_{k=0}^{K}\frac{c^{k}}{d^{k}k!},\qquad\qquad\epsilon_{\theta}=\frac{\pi}{2^{n_{\theta}}}

,

(319)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{t}=\left(\frac{2\|A\|}{\lambda_{B}}+\frac{\|A\|^{2}}{\lambda_{B}^{2}}\right)\left[2^{n_{t}}\left(e^{\lambda_{B}\tau/2^{n_{t}}}-1\right)-\lambda_{B}\tau(1+\lambda_{B}\tau/2^{n_{t}+1})\right].

(320)

Proof.

The complete interaction picture simulation is indicated in

and the clock preparation shown in

).

and the block encoding of

are given in entries 8 and 9 of

Provided
\lambda_{B}t
is rational, for any integer
\mathcal{N}
,
\lambda_{B}t/\mathcal{N}
must be rational, and we can choose
c
and
d
to satisfy
c/d=\lambda_{B}\tau
for
\tau=t/\mathcal{N}
.
Then (
317
) comes from (
311
), and constrains the value of
c/d
.
Since
c/d
is set by
\mathcal{N}
, we need a way to select
\mathcal{N}
so that
c/d
satisfies (
317
).
Choosing some value of
b_{r}
we can find a lower bound
P_{\min}
on
{\rm Ps}\left(\Sigma(0),b_{r}\right)
(for example as given in
Appendix
J
below).
Then we can set
\mathcal{N}=\lceil\lambda_{B}t/\ln(2P_{\min})\rceil
, and
\lambda_{B}\tau=\lambda_{B}t/\mathcal{N}
is strictly less than
\ln(2P_{\min})
.
Then
c/d=\lambda_{B}\tau<\ln(2P_{\min})
implies that (
317
) is satisfied.

The error bound in (
318
) is from (
315
).
The quantities
\epsilon_{K}
,
\epsilon_{\theta}
and
\epsilon_{t}
are bounds on the error due to truncating the Dyson series, the rotation on the ancilla qubit for the amplitude amplification, and the time discretisation, respectively.
The norm of each successive term in the Dyson series is bounded as
(\tau\lambda_{B})^{k}/k!
, and
\lambda_{B}\tau=c/d
.
Therefore the error in truncating the Dyson series to order
K
can be bounded by the error in truncating the exponential of
c/d
, giving the expression for
\epsilon_{K}
in (
319
).
The expression for
\epsilon_{\theta}
in (
319
) is from (
313
).
The bound in (
320
) is from (
293
), where we have used
\lambda_{B}
as the upper bound on
\|B\|
.
∎

The algorithm will be most efficient when
c/d
is a close rational approximation of
\ln 2
, so
\mathcal{N}
is minimized.
This is because
c/d
is upper bounded by
\ln[2{\rm Ps}\left(\Sigma(0),b_{r}\right)]
according to (
317
),
{\rm Ps}\left(\Sigma(0),b_{r}\right)
is close to 1, and
\mathcal{N}=\lambda_{B}td/c
.
It would be expected that for a real application one can choose the exact time to be simulated, so that
c
and
d
are relatively small (for example
9/13
is less than but close to
\ln 2
).
If evolution for a specific
t
is needed where
\lambda_{B}t
is not rational, we prepare an ancilla register in the state

\cos\theta_{B}\lvert 0\rangle+\sin\theta_{B}\lvert 1\rangle

(321)

and use the amplitude
\cos\theta_{B}
to artificially adjust the normalization factor
\lambda_{B}
. This completes our costing of the interaction picture algorithm for generic Hamiltonians.

Appendix J
Bound on the success probability for preparing equal superposition

Here we consider the probability of success for preparation of an equal superposition state and place a lower bound on it for given
b_{r}
.
The probability for success was given in
Sanders et al. 2020
, and is

{\rm Ps}\left(n,b_{r}\right)=\frac{n}{2^{\lceil\log n\rceil}}\left[\left(1+\left(2-\frac{4n}{2^{\lceil\log n\rceil}}\right)\sin^{2}\theta(n,b_{r})\right)^{2}+\sin^{2}(2\theta(n,b_{r}))\right],

(322)

where
\theta(n,b_{r})
is the angle of rotation on an ancilla qubit.
It turns out that there is a simple lower bound for the success probability, though the expression for
\theta
is adjusted slightly from the formula used before. The result is as in the following theorem.

Theorem 13
.

For all
n,b_{r}\in\mathbb{N}
, taking

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{2^{\lceil\log n\rceil}},

(323)

\displaystyle\theta_{0}

\displaystyle=\arcsin\left(\frac{1}{2\sqrt{x}}\right),

(324)

\displaystyle\theta(n,b_{r})

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi}{2^{b_{r}}}{\rm round}\left[\frac{2\pi}{2^{b_{r}}}\left(\theta_{0}-\frac{(\pi/2^{b_{r}})^{2}(2x-1)}{\sqrt{4x-1}}\right)\right]

(325)

gives the success probability for preparation of an equal superposition state lower bounded as

{\rm Ps}\left(n,b_{r}\right)\geq 1-\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{\pi}{2^{b_{r}}}\right)^{2}.

(326)

Proof.

Now with
b_{r}=1
or
b_{r}=2
the result is trivial, because the right-hand side is negative.
We therefore aim to prove the result for
b_{r}\geq 3
.
Because the expression for
{\rm Ps}\left(n,b_{r}\right)
depends only on the ratio
x=n/2^{\lceil\log n\rceil}
, we can prove the lower bound for all values of
x
in the range
x\in(1/2,1)
, and that will give us the result for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then the probability of success can be written as

{\rm Ps}\left(n,b_{r}\right)=x\left[\left(1+\left(2-4x\right)\sin^{2}\theta\right)^{2}+\sin^{2}(2\theta)\right].

(327)

Let us take
\Delta\theta=\theta-\theta_{0}
.
Using elementary trigonometric identities, the probability of success can be rewritten as

\displaystyle{\rm Ps}\left(n,b_{r}\right)

\displaystyle=1-4(1-x)(4x-1)\sin^{2}(\Delta\theta)

\displaystyle\quad+4(1-x)\left[2(4x-1-2x^{2})\sin^{2}(\Delta\theta)-(2x-1)\sqrt{4x-1}\sin(2\Delta\theta)\right]\sin^{2}(\Delta\theta).

(328)

To prove lower bounds for
x\in(1/2,1)
, we use

\displaystyle(2x-1)(3-x)

\displaystyle\geq 0

\displaystyle(2x-1)(3-x)+2

\displaystyle>0

\displaystyle 4(1-x)(2x-1)[(2x-1)(3-x)+2]

\displaystyle\geq 0

\displaystyle 4(1-x)(2x-1)[(2x-1)(3-x)+2]+1

\displaystyle>0

\displaystyle\left[2(4x-1-2x^{2})\right]^{2}-\left[(2x-1)\sqrt{4x-1}\right]^{2}

\displaystyle>0.

(329)

Next,
4x-1-2x^{2}=(2x-1)(3/2-x)+1/2
is positive for
x\in(1/2,1)
, and
2x-1
and
4x-1
are non-negative.
As a result, we have

2(4x-1-2x^{2})>(2x-1)\sqrt{4x-1}.

(330)

We also find that

\sin^{2}\Delta\theta-\left[\sin(2\Delta\theta)-2\Delta\theta\right]=(\Delta\theta)^{2}+\mathcal{O}((\Delta\theta)^{3}),

(331)

so it is non-negative for small
\Delta\theta
.
Moreover this expression has zeros of its derivative for
n\pi
and
n\pi-\arctan(1/2)
.
This means the only zeros of its derivative for
\Delta\theta\in[-0.7,0.7]
are at 0 and
-\arctan(1/2)\approx-0.46
.
This means that the function
\sin^{2}\Delta\theta-\left[\sin(2\Delta\theta)-2\Delta\theta\right]
can turn around towards negative values for
\Delta\theta<-0.46
, but computing it at
\Delta\theta=0.7
gives about
0.000466
, which is still positive.
This means that for
\Delta\theta\in[-0.7,0.7]
we have

\sin^{2}\Delta\theta-\left[\sin(2\Delta\theta)-2\Delta\theta\right]\geq 0,

(332)

because negative values would require an additional turning point.
That implies

2(4x-1-2x^{2})\sin^{2}(\Delta\theta)\geq(2x-1)\sqrt{4x-1}\left[\sin(2\Delta\theta)-2\Delta\theta\right],

(333)

which in turn implies

2(4x-1-2x^{2})\sin^{2}(\Delta\theta)-(2x-1)\sqrt{4x-1}\sin(2\Delta\theta)\geq-2(2x-1)\sqrt{4x-1}\Delta\theta

(334)

for
x\in(1/2,1)
and
\Delta\theta\in[-0.7,0.7]
.
This means that the probability is lower bounded by

\displaystyle{\rm Ps}\left(n,b_{r}\right)

\displaystyle\geq 1-4(1-x)(4x-1)\sin^{2}(\Delta\theta)-8(1-x)(2x-1)\sqrt{4x-1}\Delta\theta\,\sin^{2}(\Delta\theta)

\displaystyle=1-4(1-x)\left[(4x-1)+2(2x-1)\sqrt{4x-1}\Delta\theta\right]\sin^{2}(\Delta\theta).

(335)

Next, we have for
x\in(1/2,1)
and
\Delta\theta\in[-0.7,0.7]

\displaystyle 4(1-x)(3x-1)

\displaystyle\geq 0

\displaystyle 4x-1-3(2x-1)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq 0

\displaystyle\sqrt{4x-1}

\displaystyle\geq\sqrt{3}(2x-1)

\displaystyle 4x-1

\displaystyle\geq\sqrt{3}(2x-1)\sqrt{4x-1}

\displaystyle 4x-1

\displaystyle\geq-2(2x-1)\sqrt{4x-1}\Delta\theta.

(336)

As a result, we can lower bound the success probability by

{\rm Ps}\left(n,b_{r}\right)\geq 1-4(1-x)\left[(4x-1)+2(2x-1)\sqrt{4x-1}\Delta\theta\right](\Delta\theta)^{2}.

(337)

Note also that the derivative of this expression with respect to
\Delta\theta
is

-8(1-x)\left[(4x-1)+3(2x-1)\sqrt{4x-1}\Delta\theta\right]\Delta\theta.

(338)

Now
4x-1\geq\sqrt{3}(2x-1)\sqrt{4x-1}
implies the expression in square brackets must be non-negative for
\Delta\theta\in[-1/\sqrt{3},1/\sqrt{3}]
.
This implies that, provided we are considering
\Delta\theta\in[-1/\sqrt{3},1/\sqrt{3}]
, we can lower bound the probability of success in any region by the probabilities of success at the bounds of the region.

Now, using the formula in the theorem for
\theta
, we have

\Delta\theta+\frac{(\pi/2^{b_{r}})^{2}(2x-1)}{\sqrt{4x-1}}\in[-\pi/2^{b_{r}},\pi/2^{b_{r}}].

(339)

Note that
\sqrt{4x-1}\geq\sqrt{3}(2x-1)
implies

\frac{2x-1}{\sqrt{4x-1}}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}.

(340)

Therefore the maximal value of
\Delta\theta
with
{b_{r}}\geq 3
is

\frac{(\pi/8)^{2}}{\sqrt{3}}+\pi/2^{8}\approx 0.481734<\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}.

(341)

Because
\Delta\theta\in[-1/\sqrt{3},1/\sqrt{3}]
, we can lower bound the probability by the lower bounds at the extremal points in the range of
\Delta\theta
, which give

\displaystyle{\rm Ps}\left(n,b_{r}\right)

\displaystyle\geq 1-4(1-x)(4x-1)\left(\frac{\pi}{2^{b_{r}}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+(2x-1)^{2}(1-x)\left(\frac{\pi}{2^{b_{r}}}\right)^{4}\pm\frac{24(2x-1)^{3}(1-x)}{\sqrt{4x-1}}\left(\frac{\pi}{2^{b_{r}}}\right)^{5}+\frac{8(2x-1)^{4}(1-x)}{4x-1}\left(\frac{\pi}{2^{b_{r}}}\right)^{6}.

(342)

Next, we have that
\sqrt{4x-1}\geq\sqrt{3}(2x-1)
implies

\displaystyle 20(2x-1)^{2}(1-x)\sqrt{4x-1}

\displaystyle\geq 20\sqrt{3}(2x-1)^{3}(1-x)

\displaystyle 20(2x-1)^{2}(1-x)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{24(2x-1)^{3}(1-x)}{\sqrt{4x-1}}

\displaystyle(2x-1)^{2}(1-x)\left(\frac{\pi}{2^{b_{r}}}\right)^{4}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{24(2x-1)^{3}(1-x)}{\sqrt{4x-1}}\left(\frac{\pi}{2^{b_{r}}}\right)^{5}

(343)

because
\pi/2^{b_{r}}<1
.
Hence we have

(2x-1)^{2}(1-x)\left(\frac{\pi}{2^{b_{r}}}\right)^{4}\pm\frac{24(2x-1)^{3}(1-x)}{\sqrt{4x-1}}\left(\frac{\pi}{2^{b_{r}}}\right)^{5}\geq 0,

(344)

and the sixth-order term in (
J
) is non-negative, so we obtain the lower bound

{\rm Ps}\left(n,b_{r}\right)\geq 1-4(1-x)(4x-1)\left(\frac{\pi}{2^{b_{r}}}\right)^{2}.

(345)

Now
(1-x)(4x-1)
has its maximal value of
9/16
for
x=5/8
, so the lower bound becomes

{\rm Ps}\left(n,b_{r}\right)\geq 1-\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{\pi}{2^{b_{r}}}\right)^{2}

(346)

as required.
∎

If we take
b_{r}=8
for example, then the probability of success must be at least
0.999661
.
That is useful for the general interaction picture simulations described in
Appendix
I
, because then
\ln[2{\rm Ps}\left(\Sigma(0),8\right)]>0.6928
.
That is larger than
9/13
, so one can use
c/d=9/13
as a good approximation of
\ln 2
and still satisfy (
317
).

Appendix K
Simulating first quantized real space grid representations

Whether the goal is to perform non-Born-Oppenheimer dynamics or to solve the electronic structure problem, in order to simulate molecular or material systems on a quantum computer one must discretize the wavefunction and map it to qubits. Perhaps the most obvious representation involves projecting the wavefunction onto a real space grid and encoding the positions of each particle explicitly in a quantum register. In this case, the computational basis states that span the wavefunction of interest might be stored in the quantum computer as
\lvert r_{1}r_{2}\cdots r_{\eta}R_{1}R_{2}\cdots R_{L}\rangle
where the
r_{i}
and
R_{\ell}
index the coordinates of the
\eta
electrons and
L
nuclei, respectively, using binary registers with
N
grid points (each requiring
\log N
qubits). Thus, this encoding would require
(\eta+L)\log N
qubits.

Representing the potential part of the Hamiltonian is straightforward when using a real space grid (the operators
U
,
V
and
V_{\rm nuc}
would take the same diagonal forms as they do in (
1
)). However, representing the kinetic operators will require some thought. One can see that if basis functions are not overlapping in space (e.g., delta functions or finite elements with disjoint support) the integrals from the Galerkin discretization would suggest that the kinetic operator is also diagonal. To avoid this, one idea would be to take a finite-difference approach and approximate the Laplacian using a
k
-point stencil; this is exactly the approach taken in first quantization in
Kivlichan et al. 2017
and described in second quantization in Appendix A of
Babbush et al. 2018b
. Another idea, similar to using what is referred to as a “discrete value representation” (DVR)
Light and Carrington Jr 2000
, would be to exactly define the kinetic operator in its eigenbasis and approximate a transformation that relates the grid representation to the eigenbasis of the kinetic operator. For example, if using a regularly spaced grid then one might make the approximation that the quantum Fourier transform (QFT) diagonalizes the kinetic operator. After all, the kinetic operator is diagonal in the momentum basis (plane waves) and plane waves are obtained as the continuous Fourier transform of delta functions. However, this is an approximation when using a finite grid; the QFT is a discrete Fourier transform (which is linear) and a linear combination of
N
plane waves cannot sum to a delta function. This sort of representation is employed by the first paper to consider quantum simulations of molecular systems using a grid representation by Kassal
et al.

Kassal et al. 2008
. Components of that algorithm were assessed for fault-tolerance in the work of Jones
et al.

Cody Jones et al. 2012
but that work stopped short of estimating all of the constant factors associated with the algorithm realization.

In addition to the necessity of the approximations already mentioned there are other unfavorable characteristics of real space grid representations for use in quantum simulation. For example, basis errors are no longer variationally bounded when deviating from a Galerkin discretization and extrapolations to the continuum limit might be less reliable. Furthermore, ways of softening the potential and removing core electrons (akin to pseudopotentials for plane wave methods) are less developed for grids. Thus, the majority of work on simulating molecular systems focuses on Galerkin discretizations involving basis functions rather than grids. Nonetheless, in this section we will briefly describe how one can block encode a real space representation of the Hamiltonian with the same asymptotic complexity as the main approaches pursued in this paper. Since these grid representations are not periodic, it is possible that they might converge faster than plane waves when simulating non-periodic systems. Also, since the block encoding algorithms are entirely different, it is possible that this approach might have lower constant factors.

We can express the aforementioned first quantized real space Hamiltonian as

\displaystyle H_{\rm BO}

\displaystyle=T+U+V+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell\neq\kappa=1}^{L}\frac{\zeta_{\ell}\zeta_{\kappa}}{\left\|R_{\ell}-R_{\kappa}\right\|}\qquad\qquad H_{\rm non-BO}=T+T_{\rm nuc}+U_{\rm non-BO}+V+V_{\rm nuc}

(347)

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\eta}{\rm QFT}_{i}\left(\sum_{p\in G}\frac{\left\|k_{p}\right\|^{2}}{2}\lvert p\rangle\!\langle p\rvert_{i}\right){\rm QFT}_{i}^{\dagger}\qquad T_{\rm nuc}=\sum_{\ell=\eta+1}^{L+\eta}{\rm QFT}_{\ell}\left(\sum_{p\in G}\frac{\left\|k_{p}\right\|^{2}}{2\,m_{\ell}}\lvert p\rangle\!\langle p\rvert_{\ell}\right){\rm QFT}_{\ell}^{\dagger}

(348)

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{\eta}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\sum_{p\in G}\frac{\zeta_{\ell}}{\left\|R_{\ell}-r_{p}\right\|}\lvert p\rangle\!\langle p\rvert_{i}\qquad\quad U_{\rm non-BO}=-\sum_{i=1}^{\eta}\sum_{\ell=\eta+1}^{L+\eta}\sum_{p,q\in G}\frac{\zeta_{\ell}}{2\left\|r_{p}-r_{q}\right\|}\lvert p\rangle\!\langle p\rvert_{i}\lvert q\rangle\!\langle q\rvert_{\ell}

(349)

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\neq j=1}^{\eta}\sum_{p,q\in G}\frac{1}{2\left\|r_{p}-r_{q}\right\|}\lvert p\rangle\!\langle p\rvert_{i}\lvert q\rangle\!\langle q\rvert_{j}\qquad V_{\rm nuc}=\sum_{\ell\neq\kappa=\eta+1}^{L+\eta}\sum_{p,q\in G}\frac{\zeta_{\ell}\zeta_{\kappa}}{2\left\|r_{p}-r_{q}\right\|}\lvert p\rangle\!\langle p\rvert_{\ell}\lvert q\rangle\!\langle q\rvert_{\kappa}

(350)

where
{\rm QFT}_{i}
is the usual quantum Fourier transform applied to register
i
8
8

8

Again, we emphasize that
T
is only approximately given by the expression involving the QFT. This relation is exact in the continuum limit. For finite sized grids, it cannot be the case that the QFT completely diagonalizes the momentum operator.
. As in the main text, we have used atomic units where
\hbar
as well as the mass and charge of the electron are unity and
\|\cdot\|
denotes a function taking the 2-norm of its argument. In the above expression
\ell
and
\kappa
index nuclear degrees of freedom; thus,
R_{\ell}
represents the positions of nuclei and
\zeta_{\ell}
the atomic numbers of nuclei. Furthermore, we have the following definition of grid points and their frequencies in the dual space defined by the QFT:

r_{p}=\frac{p\,\Omega^{1/3}}{N^{1/3}}\qquad\qquad k_{p}=\frac{2\pi p}{\Omega^{1/3}}\qquad\qquad p\in G\qquad\qquad G=\left[-\frac{N^{1/3}-1}{2},\frac{N^{1/3}-1}{2}\right]^{3}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{3}\,,

(351)

where
\Omega
is the volume of our simulation cell and
N
is the number of grid points in the cell. Again, this is the same representation used in the first work on quantum simulating chemistry in first quantization, by Kassal
et al.

Kassal et al. 2008
, well over a decade ago. We note that like with the plane waves, it would be possible to use different grids for the different particles but we avoid doing that here for simplicity.

We now show that one can simulate the Hamiltonian of (
347
) with the same complexity as the approaches of this paper using either qubitization, or interaction picture simulation frameworks. Here we will discuss only the Born-Oppenheimer case for simplicity, but the techniques readily generalize to the non-Born-Oppenheimer Hamiltonian. Focusing first on the qubitization approach, the goal is to block encode the operators
T
,
U
and
V
. The block encoding of
T
can be accomplished using almost the same techniques used for plane waves. The only difference comes in the implementation of the
\mathrm{SEL}
operator. Whenever the ancilla register flags that a term from
T
should be applied, one must apply the QFT, then phase the register by the appropriate amount, then apply the inverse QFT.
The QFT needs to be performed independently on
\eta
registers each of
\log N
qubits, so has gate complexity
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\eta\log N)
, where here the
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}
indicates terms double-logarithmic in
N
are omitted
Cleve and Watrous 2000
.
That is complexity in terms of one and two-qubit gates, and requires rotations up to
\mathcal{O}(\log N)
bits of precision. The complexity in terms of Toffoli gates is
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\eta\log^{2}N)
.

Here the block encoding of
U
and
V
will deviate from the other strategies of this paper. Our approach will be to break up the terms as a sum of many terms with equal magnitude coefficients that can be computed on-the-fly and applied as phases, mirroring a strategy first introduced in
Berry et al. 2014b
.
First, we begin by re-expressing the
V
operator as

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle=\frac{v_{\rm max}}{M}\sum_{i\neq j=1}^{\eta}\sum_{p,q\in G}\sum_{m=1}^{M}v\left(m,r_{p},r_{q}\right)\lvert p\rangle\!\langle p\rvert_{i}\lvert q\rangle\!\langle q\rvert_{j}

(352)

\displaystyle v\left(m,r_{p},r_{q}\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1&\qquad\frac{m\,v_{\rm max}}{M}<\frac{1}{2\left\|r_{p}-r_{q}\right\|}\\
0&\qquad{\rm otherwise}\end{cases}

(353)

\displaystyle v_{\rm max}

\displaystyle=\textrm{max}_{p\neq q}\left(\frac{1}{2\left\|r_{p}-r_{q}\right\|}\right)=\frac{N^{1/3}}{2\,\Omega^{1/3}}\,,

(354)

where in the inequality above we take advantage of the fact that the repulsive Coulomb kernel is always greater than zero. From this definition we can see that

\left|\frac{1}{2\left\|r_{p}-r_{q}\right\|}-\frac{v_{\rm max}}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}v\left(m,r_{p},r_{q}\right)\right|\leq\frac{v_{\rm max}}{M}\,.

(355)

Hence, we can make sure that the error in the Hamiltonian coefficients is less than
\epsilon
if
M>v_{\rm max}/\epsilon
.
This error can be suppressed exponentially since the cost of the comparison test is quadratic in the number of qubits used to represent the number (because we need to compuare squares)
Sanders et al. 2020
, which is logarithmic in
M
, and thus logarithmic
1/\epsilon
.

We can now block encode
V
in the following way. We will start by applying
\mathrm{PREP}
as a series of Hadamard gates which realize the equal superposition state over the ancilla registers
\lvert i,j\rangle\lvert m\rangle
(essentially all of the complexity of our approach will enter through
\mathrm{SEL}
). Then, controlled on register
\lvert i\rangle
we will copy the momentum held in the
i^{\rm th}
system register to an ancilla register and translate it into
r_{p}
using the relation in (
351
). We will obtain
r_{q}
in the same way, but this time controlled on the register
\lvert j\rangle
. Then, we will compute
v(m,r_{p},r_{q})
and record its value in an ancilla register. Because this ancilla register is initialized to
\lvert 0\rangle
by
\mathrm{PREP}
, if it is changed to the
\lvert 1\rangle
state by
\mathrm{SEL}
then it will be removed from the linear combination of unitaries. Indeed, we wish to remove the term from the linear combination of unitaries whenever the inequality in (
353
) is false. Therefore, the final step of our procedure should be to apply a bit flip operator to the ancilla holding
v(m,r_{p},r_{q})
and to uncompute the
r_{p}
and
r_{q}
ancillae. Thus,
\mathrm{SEL}
will act with the following steps (with
p_{i}
and
p_{j}
equivalent to
p
and
q
, respectively):

\displaystyle\mathrm{SEL}\lvert i,j\rangle\lvert m\rangle\lvert 0\rangle^{\otimes{\cal O}(\log\left(N/\epsilon\right)}\underbrace{\lvert p_{0}\cdots p_{i}\cdots p_{j}\cdots p_{\eta}\rangle}_{\lvert\psi\rangle}

\displaystyle\mapsto\lvert i,j\rangle\lvert m\rangle\lvert r_{p_{i}},r_{p_{j}}\rangle\lvert 0\rangle\lvert p_{0}\cdots p_{i}\cdots p_{j}\cdots p_{\eta}\rangle

(356)

\displaystyle\mapsto\lvert i,j\rangle\lvert m\rangle\lvert r_{p_{i}},r_{p_{j}}\rangle\lvert v\left(m,r_{p_{i}},r_{p_{j}}\right)\rangle\lvert p_{0}\cdots p_{i}\cdots p_{j}\cdots p_{\eta}\rangle

\displaystyle\mapsto\lvert i,j\rangle\lvert m\rangle\lvert 0\rangle^{\otimes{\cal O}(\log\left(N/\epsilon\right))}\lvert\neg v\left(m,r_{p_{i}},r_{p_{j}}\right)\rangle\lvert p_{0}\cdots p_{i}\cdots p_{j}\cdots p_{\eta}\rangle\,

where
\neg
implies the negation of the following bit.

One might be concerned that we would need to compute a reciprocal and a square root as part of the 2-norm in the inequality for
v(m,r_{p},r_{q})
. However, we can much more efficiently compute (
353
) by multiplying the norm through to the other side and squaring it; in other words we can evaluate
v(m,r_{p},r_{q})
by testing

4\,m^{2}v_{\rm max}^{2}\left\|r_{p}-r_{q}\right\|^{2}<M^{2}\,.

(357)

The cost of evaluating this expression is also
\mathcal{O}(\log^{2}M)=\mathcal{O}(\log^{2}(1/\epsilon))
gates using the result of
Lemma
9
, which again only contributes a polylogarithmic contribution to the gate complexity. Apart from simplicity, this form of a comparison test is much easier (will have lower constant factors) to implement because the squared 2-norm is equivalent to just computing the sum of squares; whereas the reciprocal square root can be a costly function to evaluate directly
Soeken et al. 2017
;
Häner et al. 2018
. We note that another approach to avoiding the computation of reciprocal square roots in the context of quantum simulating first quantized real space Hamiltonians is discussed in
Poirier and Jerke 2021
, where it suggested that one take advantage of structure arising from the inverse Laplace transformed version of the Coulomb kernel represented in a Cartesian component-separated approach. While that approach may have some advantages for product formula based methods, it is unclear whether it might also be useful for further reducing the cost of block encoding the Coulomb operator.

The block encoding of
U
follows a similar pattern. In this case, we will re-express the
U
operator as

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle=-\frac{u_{\rm max}}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{\eta}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\zeta_{\ell}\sum_{p\in G}\sum_{m=1}^{M}u\left(m,r_{p},R_{\ell}\right)\lvert p\rangle\!\langle p\rvert_{i}

(358)

\displaystyle u\left(m,r_{p},R_{\ell}\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1&\qquad{\rm if}\qquad\frac{m\,u_{\rm max}}{M}<\frac{1}{\left\|r_{p}-R_{\ell}\right\|}\\
0&\qquad{\rm otherwise}\end{cases}

(359)

\displaystyle u_{\rm max}

\displaystyle=\textrm{max}_{p,\ell}\left(\frac{1}{\left\|r_{p}-R_{\ell}\right\|}\right)={\cal O}\left(\frac{N^{1/3}}{\Omega^{1/3}}\right)\,.

(360)

where we will require that the
R_{\ell}
do not overlap with any of the grid points and we once again take advantage of the fact that the right-hand side of the inequality in
u(m,r_{p},R_{\ell})
is always greater than zero. Similar to the strategy employed for
V
, here the
\mathrm{SEL}
operation will compute
u(m,r_{p},R_{\ell})
into an ancilla that is then negated (thus, excluding the cases where
u(m,r_{p},R_{\ell})=0
from the linear combination of unitaries). But there are two main differences: (1) the value
R_{\ell}
will need to be loaded using a QROM and (2) we have not incorporated the
\zeta_{\ell}
into the definition of the inequality function that is computed as part of
\mathrm{SEL}
.

Instead, we will include the
\zeta_{\ell}
through the
\mathrm{PREP}
operation. At the same time we will directly load the
\lvert R_{\ell}\rangle
values into superposition (rather than computing it from
\ell
). Thus, controlled on a flag ancilla specifying that the
U
term is to be applied, our
\mathrm{PREP}
circuit will act as

\mathrm{PREP}\lvert 0\rangle^{\otimes{\cal O}\left(\log\left(N/\epsilon\right)\right)}\mapsto\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\sum_{i=1}^{\eta}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\sqrt{\frac{\zeta_{\ell}}{\eta M}}\lvert R_{\ell}\rangle\lvert i\rangle\lvert m\rangle\lvert 0\rangle\,.

(361)

Using the coherent aliasing strategies developed in
Babbush et al. 2018a
, the weighted superposition over
R_{\ell}
can be prepared with no more than
\epsilon
error in the amplitudes at Toffoli complexity
L+{\cal O}(\log(1/\epsilon))
. That will be a negligible additive cost compared to the rest of the
\mathrm{SEL}
operation which will have Toffoli complexity
{\cal O}(\eta)
for charge neutral systems. Thus, up to exponentially small errors in
\mathrm{PREP}
we can see that

\left|\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\frac{\zeta_{\ell}}{\left\|r_{p}-R_{\ell}\right\|}-\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\frac{u_{\rm max}\zeta_{\ell}}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}u\left(m,r_{p},R_{\ell}\right)\right|\leq\frac{u_{\rm max}\lambda_{\zeta}}{M}\,

(362)

with
\lambda_{\zeta}=\sum_{\ell}\zeta_{\ell}
,
and again we can make sure that the error in the Hamiltonian coefficients is less than
\epsilon
if
M>u_{\rm max}\lambda_{\zeta}/\epsilon
. Once again, this
\epsilon
will only enter the gate complexity inside of logarithms.

Therefore, we see that we can implement the block encoding of
(T+U+V)/\lambda
with gate complexity
\widetilde{\cal O}(\eta)
. Given our scheme, the
\lambda_{T}
value is exactly the same as in (
25
), and

\lambda_{V}=\eta^{2}v_{\rm max}={\cal O}\left(\frac{\eta^{2}N^{1/3}}{\Omega^{1/3}}\right)\qquad\qquad\lambda_{U}=\eta\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\zeta_{\ell}\right)u_{\rm max}={\cal O}\left(\frac{\eta^{2}N^{1/3}}{\Omega^{1/3}}\right)

(363)

where these values of
\lambda_{V}
and
\lambda_{U}
involve slightly different constant factors compared to the forms in (
25
), but the same asymptotic scaling. Accordingly, we see that the overall asymptotic complexity of the qubitization approach using this Hamiltonian would be

\widetilde{\cal O}\left(\frac{\left(\lambda_{T}+\lambda_{U}+\lambda_{V}\right)\eta}{\epsilon}\right)=\widetilde{\cal O}\left(\frac{\eta^{2}N^{2/3}}{\epsilon\,\Omega^{2/3}}+\frac{\eta^{3}N^{1/3}}{\epsilon\,\Omega^{1/3}}\right)=\widetilde{\cal O}\left(\frac{\eta^{4/3}N^{2/3}+\eta^{8/3}N^{1/3}}{\epsilon}\right)\,

(364)

for
\eta\propto\Omega
,
which is the same as in (
26
).
It is likely that the QFT will be the dominant cost in this method.
The complexity for the plane waves approach tends to be dominated by the cost of controlled selection of the momentum registers, which has complexity
\mathcal{O}(\eta\log N)
and is the only part that is polynomial in
\eta
.
The QFT has scaling as
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\eta\log^{2}N)
, which is larger and so should dominate the complexity.

Finally, we discuss how the interaction picture scheme can be combined with the real space first quantized representation in order to suppress the complexity term proportional to
N^{2/3}
. To do this, we follow a similar approach as in the main paper with the block encodings of
U
and
V
as described in this appendix. The main difference is that before evolving under the kinetic operator we must apply the QFT. The gate complexity of performing the QFT in each time step is
\widetilde{\cal O}(\eta\log^{2}N)
Toffoli gates.
A second difference is that it is no longer possible to keep the total kinetic energy in an ancilla register and update it when we block-encode
U
and
V
.
At each step the kinetic energy will need to be recomputed, which has complexity
\mathcal{O}(\eta\log^{2}N)
.
That is almost as large as the complexity scaling for the QFT. As a result there is the same asymptotic scaling as the interaction picture algorithm in the main paper up to a logarithmic factor,

\widetilde{\cal O}\left(\frac{\left(\lambda_{U}+\lambda_{V}\right)\eta}{\epsilon}\right)\widetilde{\cal O}\left(\frac{\eta^{3}N^{1/3}}{\epsilon\,\Omega^{1/3}}\right)=\widetilde{\cal O}\left(\frac{\eta^{8/3}N^{1/3}}{\epsilon}\right)\,.

(365)

Again, the complexity will be increased over the plane wave approach primarily due to the QFT.
